getting ready for bed.\n- key: S045\n  name: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\n  description: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.\n  manifestation: Even though my daughter and a woman from church have both told me I look too thin, I still panic if my skirt feels snug or the scale is up even a pound, as if I’ve somehow let myself become huge overnight.\n- key: S047\n  name: Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging\n  description: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).\n  manifestation: The next day I’ll skip breakfast and lunch or push myself to do extra laps with my walking group, not because I feel energetic, but because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n- key: S049\n  name: Eating_Large_Amount\n  description: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n  manifestation: When no one is in the kitchen, I can go through leftovers from a church supper, crackers, cheese, cookies, and whatever I baked for book club in one sitting, far more than I would ever serve myself at a meal.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start, it feels like a switch flips and I’m watching myself keep eating while part of me is saying, ‘Linda, stop now,’ but my hands just keep reaching for more.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Since last winter, there has hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to make up for it by throwing up, skipping meals, or overdoing my walking.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still get through my work at the optician’s, but I’m often preoccupied and worn down, and I’ve started avoiding some church and family meals because I’m afraid of how I’ll handle the food afterward.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and five months, since the arguing at home over routines and gatherings really picked up.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / optician_dispensing / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods or avoiding certain types of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a set list of foods I’m “allowed” to eat, if that’s what you mean. I suppose I do try to avoid things I think of as too heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s the fear that sets me off. Even when people tell me I look too thin, I panic if my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up by even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it probably doesn’t make sense, but even when my daughter or someone at church says I look too thin, I don’t really feel that way. If my skirt feels tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic and feel like I’ve suddenly become huge.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I know it probably doesn’t make sense, but even when my daughter or someone at church says I look too thin, I don’t really feel that way. I haven’t bought smaller clothes or anything like that. Even when people tell me I look too thin—my daughter has said it, and someone at church—I still get really frightened if my skirt feels tight or the scale goes up even a pound. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been worried about what I’m doing after I eat too much. I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and then I sort of pretend I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. That’s what finally made me think I needed to say something.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, it’s been nearly every week, really. I don’t think there’s been many weeks where I haven’t had at least one time where I eat in private and then try to make up for it by being sick, or sometimes by skipping meals or walking too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when people tell me I look too thin—my daughter has said it, and someone at church—I still get really frightened if my skirt feels tight or the scale goes up even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods or avoiding certain types of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a set list of foods I’m “allowed” to eat, if that’s what you mean. I suppose I do try to avoid things I think of as too heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s the fear that sets me off. Even when people tell me I look too thin, I panic if my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up by even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it probably doesn’t make sense, but even when my daughter or someone at church says I look too thin, I don’t really feel that way. If my skirt feels tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic and feel like I’ve suddenly become huge.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been keeping track of it closely enough to say it’s been significant. I haven’t bought smaller clothes or anything like that. I don’t think of myself as underweight, but I know other people might see me as thin. I don’t know what the healthy range would be for my height and age, so I wouldn’t want to guess. I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and then I sort of pretend I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. That’s what finally made me think I needed to say something.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, it’s been nearly every week, really. I don’t think there’s been many weeks where I haven’t had at least one time where I eat in private and then try to make up for it by being sick, or sometimes by skipping meals or walking too much.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when people tell me I look too thin—my daughter has said it, and someone at church—I still get really frightened if my skirt feels tight or the scale goes up even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly become huge overnight, even though part of me knows that doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit yourself to only eating certain foods or avoiding certain types of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a set list of foods I’m “allowed” to eat, if that’s what you mean. I suppose I do try to avoid things I think of as too heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s the fear that sets me off. Even when people tell me I look too thin, I panic if my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up by even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it probably doesn’t make sense, but even when my daughter or someone at church says I look too thin, I don’t really feel that way. If my skirt feels tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic and feel like I’ve suddenly become huge.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been keeping track of it closely enough to say it’s been significant. My weight does go up and down a bit, but I couldn’t give you a clear number.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your weight going down to a point where others have commented on it or you've had to buy smaller clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t bought smaller clothes or anything like that. People have said I look thin, but I can’t say I’ve noticed my weight going down in some clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than you did when you were at your normal weight as an adult?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear “normal weight” number in my head to compare it to, and I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as underweight, but I know other people might see me as thin. I just don’t have a clear number to go by.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is lower than what most doctors would consider healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know what the healthy range would be for my height and age, so I wouldn’t want to guess. I know I may look thin to some people, but I don’t know if a doctor would say it’s too low.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s the most important thing. It does matter to me, probably more than I’d like, but I don’t know if I can rank it above everything else about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would continue to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I would. It’s really tied to feeling like I’ve eaten too much and then worrying about what that means for my weight or shape. I suppose I do try to avoid things I think of as too heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really kept track of it like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. It’s the fear that sets me off. Even when people tell me I look too thin, I panic if my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up by even a pound. It feels like I’ve suddenly let myself become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are overweight even when others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it probably doesn’t make sense, but even when my daughter or someone at church says I look too thin, I don’t really feel that way. If my skirt feels tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic and feel like I’ve suddenly become huge.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been keeping track of it closely enough to say it’s been significant. My weight does go up and down a bit, but I couldn’t give you a clear number.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your weight going down to a point where others have commented on it or you've had to buy smaller clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t bought smaller clothes or anything like that. People have said I look thin, but I can’t say I’ve noticed my weight going down in some clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than you did when you were at your normal weight as an adult?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear “normal weight” number in my head to compare it to, and I haven’t been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as underweight, but I know other people might see me as thin. I just don’t have a clear number to go by.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is lower than what most doctors would consider healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know what the healthy range would be for my height and age, so I wouldn’t want to guess. I know I may look thin to some people, but I don’t know if a doctor would say it’s too low.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s the most important thing. It does matter to me, probably more than I’d like, but I don’t know if I can rank it above everything else about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would continue to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I would. It’s really tied to feeling like I’ve eaten too much and then worrying about what that means for my weight or shape. I’ve been going upstairs afterward and making myself throw up, then pretending I’m just tidying up or getting ready for bed.\n\nDoctor: Do you see yourself as fat even when you look in the mirror\n\nPatient: Not exactly all the time, but I do feel much bigger than people say I am. My daughter and a woman at church have both told me I look too thin, but if my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up even a pound, I panic. It feels like I’ve suddenly become huge overnight, even if that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I suppose logically I know people are telling me I look too thin, like my daughter and someone from church. But when I look at myself, I don’t really feel normal. If my skirt feels tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic and feel like I’ve suddenly become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the weight you think you should be?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even if other people say I look too thin, I don’t feel that way when I look at myself. If my skirt feels a bit snug or the scale has gone up even one pound, I panic, like I’ve suddenly become much bigger than I should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a doctor would say your current weight is too low for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes. I mean, my daughter and a woman at church have both said I look too thin. But I don’t really feel that way myself. If my skirt feels snug or the scale goes up even a pound, I panic like I’ve suddenly become huge overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a doctor would consider your current weight to be underweight for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I think a doctor probably would say I’m underweight, yes. I’ve had my daughter and a woman from church tell me I look too thin. But in my own head it doesn’t feel that way—if my skirt feels a bit tight or the scale is up even a pound, I panic like I’ve let myself get huge overnight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
